Trump Is Preparing for a Long War
2026-09-10

Trump Is Preparing for a Long War

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
To hear the Trump administration tell it, the war with Iran is all but over. U.S. military posture tells a different story: Naval forces are being positioned for a sustained presence at least through the end of the year, keeping open the option of escalation. The Navy is moving aircraft carriers, its signature asset, around the globe so that two can be deployed in and around the Persian Gulf in the final months of this year, giving the United States the ability to hit both Iran and Iran-backed Houthi rebels in Yemen, who have recently fired barrages of missiles at Saudi Arabia. Meanwhile, the Army has extended the deployment of elements of the 82nd Airborne Division, which are already in the region, because of “operational requirements.” The conflict, now in its seventh month, has already lasted far longer than the president’s initial predictions of victory within a few weeks. And senior officials have recently downplayed the scale of the conflict in the apparent hope that it will recede from the headlines. Vice President Vance claimed to reporters last week that the U.S. is not at war at all. Soon after, President Trump referred to the conflict as “small potatoes,” though he has since termed it a war. After Iran targeted a U.S. warship, the Navy sank five Iranian crude-oil tankers on Tuesday, according to U.S. Central Command. With no talks under way, and no end to the hostilities in sight, the Navy is staying on a war footing indefinitely, adding to the severe burden already placed on the Navy and on U.S. munitions stockpiles. The unusually long deployment of the carrier USS Abraham Lincoln, whose crew spent 264 consecutive days at sea and dropped more than 1.5 million pounds of ordnance, was plagued with food and supply shortages, plumbing problems, and declining morale. The Lincoln eventually departed for Thailand, relieved by USS George Washington. The Washington joined about 20 other ships in the Middle East, including USS George H. W. Bush, another carrier that has been there since April. Now the USS Theodore Roosevelt carrier strike group is preparing to leave its San Diego base to relieve the Washington, which is expected to finish its deployment by the end of the year. In short, the Navy is planning to maintain two aircraft carrier strike groups in the Middle East near Iran for at least the rest of 2026, U.S. officials told us. The Navy has seldom mustered such a formidable presence there since the heights of the Afghan and Iraq Wars, and the move reflects the fact that the administration is considering a ramp-up of its military-strike campaign after November, the officials said. Talking to reporters late last month, Admiral Daryl Caudle, the chief of naval operations, prepared the Roosevelt’s crew for tough times ahead and a lengthy deployment at sea, especially now that Iran’s attacks on U.S. bases in the Gulf have made getting sailors to shore much more challenging. Caudle acknowledged that he couldn’t say how long the Roosevelt’s deployment would last and that it could keep being extended. “If we know we’re going into harm’s way, we should be able to project with a little bit more fidelity how long the carrier and the strike group is going to stay on station,” Caudle said, according to USNI News. “We do these extensions in little chunks, and the families don’t see any endpoint so they can plan their own lives—that creates a lot of stress for sailors.” He added: “I typically am not a big fan of extensions, and I push back hard on that. But the enemy gets a vote.” An extended conflict is not at all what the White House wants. Trump has been trying to extricate himself from the war for months, frequently frustrated that he has not been able to bludgeon Iran into surrendering—and angry that Tehran has continued to defy his bombastic threats. In recent weeks, the administration has settled on a campaign of economic pressure to try to force Tehran to negotiate more seriously. Officials told us that there are signs that the approach is working and that Iran’s economy, already battered, is further teetering. But economic pressure can take months or years to have its full effect. And the hard-liners in Iran have long shown a willingness to ignore the suffering of their people if it means tightening their grip on power. Trump hinted at Iran’s perseverance yesterday, telling reporters before boarding Air Force One that he now believes Tehran will try to hold out until after the American elections. Some aides think it could be even longer. U.S. Central Command told us it does not talk about future troop movements. Read: The real reason Trump wants economic sanctions on Iran A sustained economic chokehold has potential political benefits for the administration, keeping the unpopular conflict quiet while Republicans run for election this fall. But the inherent complication with that plan is that the status quo would likely keep gas prices high, which is why the administration has kept such a focus on the Strait of Hormuz, a vital waterway for global energy supplies. Iran seized it, and effectively shut it, in the early days of the war. Trump has privately told advisers that he is eager to claim victory and move on, but acknowledges that he cannot credibly do so while the strait remains largely closed or menaced by Iran, a senior White House aide and an outside adviser told us on the condition of anonymity to discuss internal deliberations. The president has signed off on military action around the strait to punish Iran’s efforts to thwart the U.S. blockade. The administration has also begun preparing for the possibility that the economic-pressure campaign may push Iran into ramping up its attacks, creating the need for a greater U.S. military presence, according to the senior aide. And though Trump would prefer to be done with the war as soon as possible, he has signaled a willingness to consider renewing a more sustained military campaign—but likely only after the midterms and once the Pentagon has replaced some of its depleted weapons supply. Right now, Tehran appears to favor escalation. In recent days, Iranian forces attacked commercial shipping, threatened to create a maritime exclusion zone in the Gulf (a nonstarter for the U.S. and other Gulf countries), aimed ballistic missiles at nearby U.S. naval vessels, and vowed to “take action against any threat, even before it is carried out.” Collectively, these moves by Tehran were sending a message: We are willing to go big to get a deal. The regime has also been preparing its own citizens for extended hardship, pushing national unity amid rampant inflation and shortages brought on by U.S. sanctions. “Their message is, The U.S. is using economic pressure to create another uprising in Iran, the prelude to the next big war,” Vali Nasr, a professor of international affairs and Middle East studies at the Johns Hopkins School of Advanced International Studies, told us. “They are trying to prepare the population for no short-term resolution.” Carriers are not the only ships capable of supporting the U.S. blockade of the Strait of Hormuz. But they give the United States something the other ships cannot: a mobile airfield capable of quickly launching sustained combat operations without relying on bases in countries that could come under Iranian attack. More broadly, the carriers are considered a form of deterrence, a projection of American power and intent. Yet they can also illustrate the limits of U.S. capabilities. As long as two of the 10 available U.S. carriers are near the Gulf, the Navy can’t sustain a carrier elsewhere in the world without putting a tremendous strain on the force. The other carriers are either in shipyards or resetting between deployments. Every time the U.S. extends a deployment, that jolts scheduling for shipyards by months. And it puts strain on Navy families and sailors. That also means no aircraft carrier in the Pacific to show U.S. determination to defend its allies and Taiwan against possible Chinese aggression. And no deployed aircraft carrier in the Western Hemisphere. “Once the administration decided on the blockade as its main line of effort, they also concluded that this would be a sustained campaign,” Bryan Clark, a defense expert at Hudson Institute, told us. “But keeping carriers in the Arabian Gulf for responsive strikes will prevent sustaining carrier presence elsewhere. We may see U.S. influence erode in places like the Asia Pacific, like Japan and Taiwan.” U.S. allies are already anticipating a less consistent U.S.-carrier presence in Asia, defense officials told us, with Japan and the Philippines discussing ways to bolster their naval forces in response to the threat from China. Meanwhile, the U.S. is relying on a different mix of ships for its operations in the Indo-Pacific, including littoral-combat ships that normally support the Middle East but arrived in Singapore for maintenance shortly after the Iran war began. The Navy has been trying to move some of those ships back toward the Gulf too. Sailors on the aircraft carriers in the Gulf will face a theater shaped by Iran’s persistence. Because of Tehran’s attacks over the past six months, the U.S. has lost the infrastructure to support multiple port visits for carriers in places such as Bahrain and the United Arab Emirates. For now, until the Navy finds alternative sites for liberty breaks, in Africa and potentially as far away as Singapore, carrier strike groups will likely get two stops during extended deployments, one en route and one when homeward bound. And with fewer safe nearby ports, resupply must be done from bases farther away, such as Diego Garcia, a British territory in the central Indian Ocean. “This will be the new normal,” one former defense official explained to us. Regular aircraft-carrier deployments last six to eight months. But the extensions in the Gulf are just the latest in a string of longer deployments over the past decade, as the U.S. has sought a robust naval presence in Europe, Asia, the Middle East, and now the Western Hemisphere. USS Gerald R. Ford anchored the flotilla that pressured Caracas in the run-up to the seizure of President Nicolás Maduro in January. Read: Venezuela seems to be going … well? Those longer deployments have ripple effects throughout the service, borrowing against future time that was slotted for maintenance and training. That has steadily eroded sailor retention and readiness. “When you overuse an asset, eventually you are going to pay a price,” John Pendleton, who directed the Government Accountability Office’s military-readiness work for more than a decade, told us. The Navy has seen the effects of such strains before. In 2017, after two fatal destroyer collisions in the Pacific just two months apart, Navy officials acknowledged that an intense operational tempo contributed: Ships were repeatedly tasked to meet demands despite shortfalls in training, maintenance, and manning, leaving crews overstretched and, in some cases, exhausted. Almost a decade on, a similar risk exists in the Persian Gulf.
The Atlantic
What Lonnie Bunch Told Me
2026-09-10

What Lonnie Bunch Told Me

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Lonnie Bunch, the secretary of the Smithsonian Institution, was already slipping into the past tense when we walked together through the National Museum of American History in February. Occasionally, as we stood before an exhibit, he’d discuss his role leading the largest museum system in the world as though he had already stepped away. Even then, there wasn’t a question of if he would retire soon, but how soon. He spoke longingly of a future life in which he took fewer flights, slept in fewer hotels, spent more time with his grandchildren. He desperately wanted more time to write. He told me he still has more books in him. Now he will get the time. Bunch announced on Tuesday that he will retire at the end of the year. “I am extraordinarily proud of what we have accomplished together,” he wrote in a statement. “I leave with pride and sadness, gratitude and joy, and with enormous confidence in the people who will carry this remarkable institution forward.” Bunch said that pressure from the Trump administration played no role in his decision. That may well be true, but Donald Trump’s second term has played a disproportionate role in shaping Bunch’s final few years in charge. The 18 months that Bunch spent holding off the White House’s efforts to influence the Smithsonian reinvigorated his commitment to the institution. But it seemed to me that he was exhausted. He did not seem to relish writing memos assuring his staff and the broader public (while reminding the president) that the Smithsonian was nonpartisan and singularly guided by scholarship; he did not want to go to work under the constant threat of the federal government pulling funding; he did not want to have lunch with the president while waiting for “the sword of Damocles to come take my head off,” as he admitted during a talk at UCLA last year. He did so because that was what the moment required. He did so because he loves the Smithsonian, which has been part of his life since he first began a job at the National Air and Space Museum nearly half a century ago. But this, I thought, was not how the secretary of the Smithsonian should have to be spending his time. It all seemed like a massive distraction from the mission. I could not help but think of Toni Morrison’s observation during a 1975 keynote address at Portland State University about “the function, the very serious function of racism, which is distraction. It keeps you from doing your work. It keeps you explaining, over and over again, your reason for being.” Over and over again, Bunch was in a position of having to explain the Smithsonian’s reason for being: “At the Smithsonian, our work is driven by scholarship, accuracy and an uncompromising commitment to tell the fullness of America’s story,” he wrote to employees in response to this summer’s White House report, which accused the National Museum of American History of ideological bias. “As public servants and the keepers of this institution, we are charged with helping a nation find understanding, hope and clarity and as part of that duty, we are dedicated to excellence, reflection and growth.” Perhaps Bunch, ever the diplomat, would disagree that Trump’s social-media posts lamenting that the Smithsonian spends too much time talking about slavery distracted anyone from the mission. (Ditto Trump’s plan to place signs on the National Mall accusing a museum of being anti-American.) And although I know from speaking with multiple staffers that Trump’s attacks on the institution bolstered their commitment, time spent defending one’s work is time away from actually doing that work. Last year, when the administration first put out an executive order targeting the Smithsonian, people flocked to the Smithsonian museums, particularly the National Museum of African American History and Culture, of which Bunch was the founding director. Many people had watched what was being done to the Kennedy Center, and it seemed like the Smithsonian might be the next Washington institution to experience a Trump-world takeover. This summer has been a reminder for me of the singular role the Smithsonian plays in American life. I live in suburban Maryland, right outside of Washington, D.C., and this summer, our guest room has been occupied by a merry-go-round of guests visiting the District. Almost all of them have made sure that they got to the National Mall to explore the museums. It could be my little cousins marveling at the planes hanging overhead at the National Air and Space Museum; my friend from graduate school spending the entire day at the National Museum of African American History and Culture and feeling like there was still so much to see; my own children going to the National Zoo with their grandparents to see the giant pandas. The Smithsonian is a sprawling organization that represents the expansiveness (as well as the complexity and contradictions) of the world and the country we live in. Despite the diatribes from the administration, people yearn for that enormity and messiness. They don’t run from it. Months ago, when I spoke with the Yale historian David Blight, a longtime friend of Bunch, he told me that when Bunch left his post, he would have the opportunity to speak powerfully and personally about the moment we are in. “He could have a moment to really say something about the terrible consequences of these history wars,” he said. “And there’s really almost nobody that quite has his pulpit when he chooses to use it.” Bunch has already alluded to the fact that he won’t go quietly—that he might have more to say when he is no longer constrained by his responsibilities to the institution. Whoever succeeds him won’t have an easy task. Few historians are as respected—both within the field and by the general public—as Bunch. In the hours since he announced his retirement, I’ve received calls and messages mourning his departure, as if a beloved head of state had stepped down early. There is also a similar sense of concern. Will Bunch’s departure accelerate the White House’s attacks on the Smithsonian or quiet them? Does anyone else have the skill set to defend the museums in the way he has? Whatever is needed to lead an entity that’s caught the eye of a heavy-handed president, Bunch’s recent experience shows that the next Smithsonian secretary will need to make at least one significant sacrifice: their time.
The Atlantic
Sarah Longwell’s Playbook for Defeating MAGA
2026-09-10

Sarah Longwell’s Playbook for Defeating MAGA

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube | Overcast | Pocket Casts Donald Trump continues to insist that he is popular on the eve of an important election, even though everyone else knows he is not. “What I really want you to do is pretend, please, that I’m on the ballot. Just come and vote. It’s so important,” he said recently. In the meantime, the GOP’s first-ever midterm convention this week—headlined, of course, by Trump—has several Republican candidates sitting it out, probably because they pay attention to polls. The war in Iran, tariffs, and high gas prices have pushed his popularity rating down to about 35 percent, and even some of the MAGA faithful have been complaining. House seats that had been solidly red in Texas, Colorado, South Carolina, and a handful of other states are starting to look “soft,” as pollsters say. On paper, this presents an opportunity for Democrats to take back control of the House or Senate or both, and put forth some charismatic leaders of their own. But to do that, they would have to start behaving and talking very differently, Sarah Longwell, the publisher of The Bulwark and author of the new book How to Eat an Elephant argues. A longtime Republican, Longwell now says, “The Republican Party has to be defeated, and I think MAGA specifically, I want to defeat it and then salt the Earth with its ashes.” She’s spent the past few years conducting hundreds of focus groups with Trump supporters, and also swing voters, to try to understand better how Democrats can reach them. What she’s discovered is that many Democrats are mostly using the wrong approach, and they are missing a chance to speak to voters’ fears and deepest concerns. On this week’s Radio Atlantic, Longwell discusses how she thinks Democrats should talk about sensitive issues, such as immigration, and the words they should stop using. She outlines which Democratic leaders are doing a good job communicating and how to achieve what she calls “narrative dominance.” And she shares why—despite the contradictions, misinformation, and conspiracy theories she hears in her focus groups—she is hopeful about the American voter. The following is a transcript of the episode: Hanna Rosin: We’re less than 60 days away from the midterms, and in a rare moment of unity, the president and congressional Democrats have a shared goal: Make this election about Donald Trump. Donald Trump: What I really want you to do is pretend, please, that I’m on the ballot. Just come and vote. It’s so important. Rosin: Trump is so sure that he is the key to Republican victories that he has insisted on holding a Republican-convention-slash-Trump-rally in Dallas this week, where he will deliver a, quote, “presidential keynote,” presumably to help voters envision, when they’re in the booth, that he is on the ballot. But this whole scenario is true only in Trump’s imagination. As Democrats are well aware, and no doubt gloating over, Trump is not, in fact, very popular at the moment. His approval rating was at 33 percent at the end of August. And even MAGA Republicans are bristling at the war in Iran, tariffs, and high gas prices. Sarah Longwell:  Trump is as underwater as I’ve seen him in all of the years that I’ve been focused on politics in the Trump era, in large part because, much like George W. Bush toward the end of his time in office, Trump is fighting an unpopular war and he’s got a lousy economy. Rosin: I’m Hanna Rosin. This is Radio Atlantic. And that is Sarah Longwell. She’s the publisher of The Bulwark and author of a new book, How to Eat an Elephant: One Voter at a Time, which she describes as, quote, “the playbook for consigning MAGA-style politics to the ash heap of history (where it belongs).” Longwell is also a nerd for focus groups. She’s conducted hundreds since Trump first came into office. And she says voters’ attitudes toward the president have changed, even in just the past year. Longwell: In the beginning, when he first got into office, we would ask people how they were feeling about things, and they would say, Give him time. You know, Rome wasn’t built in a day. Joe Biden, XYZ—it’s his fault. Gotta give Trump some time. But now they’ve given him time, and they also identify very clearly that both the war in Iran and tariffs were both choices that Trump made himself. And so he is currently like an albatross around the necks of Republicans running in Senate races and in House races. I listen to these voters in focus groups. I was doing one a couple weeks ago; it was Florida Trump voters. We asked them to give him a letter grade, and C was the highest grade he got. It was just a bunch of C’s, D’s, and F’s. Rosin: Theoretically, this should be a great opportunity for Democrats. Longwell: I was looking at the Senate map 18 months ago, going, God, this map is terrible for Democrats. What are they gonna do? And now the Senate’s legitimately in play. Rosin: But—and this is a big but—just because Democrats have an opening here does not mean they are ready to jump into it. In fact, in her book, Longwell argues that many Democrats are not. From what she’s seen in focus groups, they don’t connect with voters in the right way. In fact, they alienate them a lot. We’ll talk about all that. But first, we need to know something about Longwell, who got her start in politics working for [Senator] Rick Santorum, who is a pretty conservative Republican. I wanna ask you: You’re a Republican. Why are you in the business of giving advice to Democrats? Why is that important to you right now? Longwell: I am not a Republican anymore. So as I lay out in the book, I grew up as a Republican and was sort of a John McCain Republican coming out of college. I was 18 when [President] Bill Clinton was impeached for having an affair with a woman, a girl, not much older than I was at the time. And so I got, in my very early formative years, slightly polarized against Democrats and then found [Senator] John McCain to be a really compelling figure as I sort of grew up into politics in my 20s. And I sort of hung in there with Republicans, although I was always, you know, I’m gay and I’m a moderate. I was always a moderate kind of Republican with some libertarian tendencies. But Trump was anathema to me, not just because he had a completely—I think he had a totally different set of policy beliefs from me. But much more importantly, as a matter of character, as a matter of liking America or, like—to have somebody running the country whose goal is to weaponize fear and anger to make us hate each other, I reject that. And I tried to hang in there for a couple of years and say, I’m gonna fight Trump from within the Republican Party. I tried to primary him going into the 2020 election, and tried to get Mark Cuban or [Maryland Governor] Larry Hogan or somebody to primary him. It’s actually why I started doing the focus groups in the first place, was I was trying to figure out how we could primary Trump. And then I continued by saying, Okay, well, nobody’s gonna seriously take this guy on. How do we get these disaffected Republicans to vote for a Democrat? And that was sort of my beginning of trying to figure out how to get people to vote for Democrats. As long as Donald Trump has the stranglehold on the Republican Party, there’s nothing in this party—in the Republican Party—for me. And more importantly, I believe it is an actively dangerous political party at the moment. Do I still believe in limited government, free markets, and American leadership in the world? Sure, I do, but I don’t see any reason why Democrats aren’t being more responsive on a number of those issues than Republicans are. I mean, the level of corruption they’re tolerating, it’s unfathomable. And so there’s nothing for me in the Republican Party, and I think the Republican Party has to be defeated. And I think MAGA specifically, I want to defeat it and then salt the Earth with its ashes. Longwell: (Laughs.) I know, I’m super intense about this. I think that Trump is really bad for America. I think he tries to weaponize our worst impulses. You know, he understands how to take people’s darkest impulses and amplify them, and he does that for his own power. I’ve never seen in my lifetime, and it’s hard to find characters like this in American history who, I think, ascended to the presidency while caring so little about America and her people. And I think he has made us all worse. Everything he has done has made America a less good country, and I’m desperate for change, and I’m desperate for Donald Trump to be a failure. My singular goal is that when we look back on the Trump years, everybody sort of agrees: That was a mistake. That was a failure. We never want to do that again. Rosin: So your driving belief is: There is a part of us that can remember that. I think there’s a lot of people sometimes who feel like voters are lost, they’re numb, they’ve forgotten how to be good. We’re hopelessly polarized. But that is not your view. This book is intended to find the place in the American voter where they can turn the country in a different direction. It’s essentially a hopeful book. Longwell: It is a hopeful book, and I wrote it because I’m confronted so often with the cynicism and desperation from people who think, Man, we’re done. America’s toast. It’s over. And our voters are fundamentally corrupt and bad. And that’s just not what I think. And what I think is that every—it’s kind of just part of the human condition, right? People have light and dark in them, and I tell this story at the end of the book, or I relay this parable. It’s, like, a Native American parable where a grandfather’s talking to his grandson, and he says, Look, inside every one of us, there’s two wolves. There’s a good wolf that represents truth and light and decency, and there’s a bad wolf that’s avarice and greed and hatred, and those two wolves are always in tension. And the grandson says, Well, which one wins? And he says, The one you feed. And Trump feeds the bad parts of America, and they’re there, those bad, dark parts. But the good parts are there too. And the question is: How do you amplify that? How do you get to a place where—both from a communication standpoint, a leadership standpoint, a political standpoint—that you are sort of feeding the good wolf? And not in a schlocky way. I’m not looking for a minister or somebody who’s perfect. We just need somebody who has the best interests of America at heart and is willing to fight for her and is willing to speak to people like they have the capacity for good again. And the other thing that I just I believe strongly is: If you think that America is finished, and you think there’s nothing left to fight for, well, then it is finished, right? If you don’t think we have agency to change things, then, yeah, it’s done. And so I really sort of reject and push back against this idea that it’s a fait accompli, and I kinda wanna shake everybody else out of that as well, because I don’t think it’s true. I think there is a lot to fight for. I think there’s a lot of good ways to do it. And frankly, I think, as we speak, there are leaders that are emerging that are coming closer to meeting the moment than we’ve seen in a while. Rosin: Okay, so let’s get into it, what the details are of how to do this. You found a lot of answers in focus groups. You’re, like, an evangelist for focus groups. Why? Like, what was the light bulb that went off for you in the early days that made you think, Oh, we can learn from these small groups of people who are sometimes frustrating and contradictory and all of those different things, but the answer is here? Longwell: Part of it was that Donald Trump was so jarring to me, such a surprise to me, that I realized that I was missing something important if America could elect somebody like Donald Trump. And so I went and started listening to voters, yes, in part because I wanted to primary him, but also I was trying to understand. And it was, like, one of those just scales-falling-from-your-eyes moments, as I listened to voters talk about why they liked Donald Trump. Longwell: Well, I mean, one of the craziest things that jumped out at me right from the beginning was how many people had watched The Apprentice. You forget about this, but Donald Trump had been living in people’s living rooms for years. And this is a really key part in why I’m optimistic. Donald Trump himself is somebody who has completely hollowed out the Republican Party and turned it into a cult of personality. So what happens when he’s gone? But he has a unique relationship with voters that other people can’t emulate, because they didn’t go on Howard Stern a thousand times, or go on Oprah all the time, or have their own talk show where they were in people’s living rooms as a carefully curated businessman that made voters believe, Oh, this guy is a good executive. He will know how to do these things. I looked at Trump and thought he was a buffoon. I looked at Trump and thought, I can’t imagine how somebody would take this person seriously. And I think it’s, like, an easy thing to forget about how Trump had fashioned himself as having a parasocial relationship with voters, even before that was kind of a thing. Rosin: And so what is the light bulb there? It’s like, okay, you learn that he’s fixed in their heads as a good businessman, despite the million facts that you could throw at them saying he tanked a casino; he tanked this or that business. It doesn’t matter. There’s no fact-check, right? Like, what are you learning? Longwell: So what I learned was that people were desperate for somebody who wasn’t a regular politician, who they saw as transcending politics. A lot of times, we try to put it in the frame of: How could these Republicans vote for this New York liberal? He gave Hillary Clinton money, and they were like, Yeah, no, that’s good, actually. He transcends politics. And also, one of the craziest things was, you know, you think about Trump as being sort of—people now say he’s far right or authoritarian. These voters saw him as a moderate because he wasn’t religious. He wasn’t like [Vice President] Mike Pence. He didn’t talk about abortion all the time, and in fact, voters would tell you over and over again, they’re like, Yeah, that guy’s not pro-life. You know, he’s probably paid for some abortions. And you just hear, for voters, that they thought of him as somebody who was more moderate, which is, I think, not how sort of the conventional framing was working. The other thing that was wild to me is: A lot of my friends or people that I knew in the media, they would say, Well, how do voters respond to this crazy thing that Trump said? And I got the opportunity in the focus groups to say—and I’ll give you an example; it’s one that I give in the book—is I was in Columbus, Ohio, the day after Trump had had the meeting in Helsinki with [Russian President] Vladimir Putin, where he sided against America’s intelligence community and with Putin, onstage, in Helsinki. Trump: They said they think it’s Russia. I have President Putin; he just said it’s not Russia. I’ll say this: I don’t see any reason why it would be. Longwell: Washington was beside itself. The whole city was, like, en fuego over 80 years of foreign policy being turned on its head. And I went into the group thinking, Okay, these are people who are gonna be Cold Warrior Reaganites. They are gonna be blown away. They’re gonna hate the fact that Donald Trump just sided against America and sided with Putin, and I was excited. And the moderator says, What did people think about what Trump said in Helsinki? And everybody just was like, What are you talking about? I have no idea what you’re talking about. And that, to me, the chasm between voters—they had no idea. Like, we had to explain it to them, and even then they’re like, I don’t know, I guess he has to meet with world leaders. The disconnect between what people out in the world cared about and what people in Washington cared about, that was a deep chasm. And it really was illuminating for me in thinking about: What are voters actually paying attention to? And the answer is: Things that affect them personally, is basically the easiest answer I can give you. Rosin: After the break: how the Democrats need to start talking differently, communicating with potential voters in a whole new way. Rosin: So let’s take an issue like immigration. That’s a complicated issue. I wrote down one of the quotes from your book. It’s from Matt from Indiana. Matt says, “The border issue brings drugs in, that’s a big issue, fentanyl. I mean, I don’t know how many times I’ve had to Narcan somebody because of fentanyl.” Can you parse that comment for me? And then, what is the advice that would come out of that comment? Because there’s a lot of confused information in that comment, and yet you would say that Matt takes it personally. Like, he connects whatever’s happening at the border with having had to Narcan people. So what do you do with something like that? Longwell: Well, in the group, I don’t try to push back on that, because I’m not trying to change Matt’s mind. But what I learned about immigration, and I’m gonna say a quote that you’ve heard a bunch of times from Donald Trump, but it gets at something very important. He says—when he was talking about immigration, he says, “They’re bringing drugs.” Trump: They’re bringing drugs. They’re bringing crime. They’re rapists. And some, I assume, are good people. Longwell: But what he is doing in that is: He is taking a bunch of different issues for people—people’s worries about drugs, people’s worries about their personal safety—and he’s saying, And immigrants are a threat to this. I call it “schema activation” in the book, but he’s basically making people afraid. He took immigration, he connected it to Your economic well-being is being hurt by immigrants. There is more crime because of immigrants. There’s more drugs because of immigrants. And so if you care about those things, as many voters do, Trump is able to tell you one complete story and put immigrants at the center of it. And I hear it back from the voters in comments like that. So what Matt is saying, it’s like, fentanyl isn’t coming over the border from Mexico. Rosin: My initial instinct is to fact-check Matt, is to just say, Matt, that’s incorrect. Fentanyl is not coming over the border. But I’m sure you would say that is a terrible way as a candidate to go about things. Why? Longwell: And that was the initial instinct of everybody, was, Well, we’re gonna fact-check all these things. Longwell: Everybody hired fact-checkers ’cause Trump was lying all the time. It’s like, Wow, we’re gonna fact-check. But voters were like, Well, who fact-checks the fact-checkers? Why should I believe the fact-checkers? And I think that the bigger thing to do is to recognize that Donald Trump saying he wanted to build a wall had nothing to do with a physical wall. It’s to understand that what Trump was doing was projecting that he cared about immigration, and he was gonna take it seriously. You don’t see any voters mad at him that the wall never got built, because the physical wall wasn’t the point. Interestingly, [Mayor Zohran] Mamdani, in New York, was talking about affordability, and he talked about his plan for grocery stores and transportation—he’s gonna make things free or low cost. I look at those policies and I think, Well, those policies are stupid, but focusing on affordability is very smart. And his ideas are like, Well, I’m gonna take affordability seriously. I’m gonna talk about the ways in which I’m gonna try to make things less expensive for you. That was the key. And so what politicians have to stop doing is fighting over the small stuff, and go right at the idea that voters care about immigration, and they think Republicans are gonna be better on it. And I think that they should, No. 1, you have to condemn Trump’s excesses on immigration, because he is committing many of them, and voters are frustrated with it, while also saying, simultaneously, as a contrast point, I will keep you safe. I care about your safety. I will make sure the border is secure. We will have law and order. We will have fairness. Because fairness is something that comes up in immigration all the time from voters, where they say, Look, I want immigrants to come into the country, but I want them to do—and this phrase, you hear it over and over and over again—I want them to do it the right way. And that is not an irrational thing to think. And so Democrats do have to find a way to sort of say, We care about law and order. We care about not having people come over the border, so we don’t know who’s in the country. That’s kind of table ante for this discussion. But also, We want immigrants to come here, because most Americans do. Most Americans just want them to come the right way. And look, there’s all kinds of racism and nativism and stuff that goes into these arguments, but as the broadest matter, and this is where I think sometimes Democrats really do—they’re like, Well, I’m gonna talk about the nativism. I wanna talk about the racism, the soft bigotry in here. And I say: Or don’t. Or just tell Americans, Hey, look, your safety really matters to me. We’re gonna keep you safe, and we’re gonna figure out how to attract the best and brightest to this country. We’re gonna figure out how to reform our immigration system so people can come here the right way. Like, that is how to talk about it. Rosin: One thing that stuck out to me in the book is when you said, If I could give Democrats one piece of advice, it is “stop talking about democracy.” ’Cause it’s true. Opponents of Trump do talk about democracy all the time, and it’s frustrating that that doesn’t matter. But it’s too abstract, or it just doesn’t break through. Longwell: Well, we talk about democracy because, like a lot of Democrats’ core constituents now, we’re all these college-educated voters who have taken a political-science class. And we understand that democracy is this term, that other countries have democracies too, and there are different democracies. Voters don’t experience democracy. They experience America. And the way that they experience democracy is—I can’t remember who I’m quoting here, but—it’s not by its roots but by its fruits, right? So when they experience democracy, democracy is voting. Democracy is freedom of speech. I don’t know; it’s just, like, the air we breathe. It’s the water that we swim in. That’s what it’s like for voters. And so when people say “democracy,” they’re like, “I don’t know what you’re—what is that?” Instead, you say, and I give this advice to politicians all the time, just stop saying “democracy,” and say “America.” And Republicans actually do this much better, right? Republicans make free speech the issue, not democracy. And that’s something that’s easier for voters to get their heads around. Rosin: Okay, let’s get practical. It’s before the midterms, then comes 2028. As you look around the landscape, are there Democrats that you see out there who are doing some of these things well—like, who are picking up this message, starting to use the right language in the way that you would want them to? Longwell: Yeah, there’s four people that I look at who I think are emerging as really strong communicators: [Senator] Jon Ossoff, [former Transportation Secretary] Pete Buttigieg, [Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez], and Mamdani. And there’s some other ones, but there’s a lot of Democrats that I look at that sort of come up from time to time where I’m like, Yeah, that person’s really good on paper. Like [Senator] Mark Kelly, good on paper. [Illinois Governor] J. B. Pritzker, good on paper. And I liked [Maryland Governor] Wes Moore. But there are people right now who are getting out there and trying different ways of communicating with voters. So Jon Ossoff, his background is as a documentary filmmaker, so he has people shooting him from underneath and from below, so he looks up there presidentially. He’s up there president-maxxing. He hit the gym, and he looks like a pre-brain-worm [Robert F.] Kennedy [Jr.], you know? He’s handsome, and he’s got a theory of communications. And then he posts the clips from his speeches. He does the speeches. Now he’s actively running, but he posts them on social media, and I’m like, Okay, that is a theory of the case about how to communicate and build relationships with voters. Pete Buttigieg is out there stumping for absolutely everybody. He stands in the middle of these town halls with a microphone. He lets people ask him any question that they want, and he engages with them sincerely and deeply and meaningfully. AOC understands social media. She understands how to be a real person. Anybody who gets her in front of a camera, she seems like an authentic, natural person. And even if you don’t like her policies—like, AOC and I probably don’t necessarily share a lot of the same policy beliefs, but I think that she is a good, kind person whose heart is in the right place about what she wants to do for America, and that counts for an awful lot with voters. And then Mamdani, like I said, I think he’s really showing the way on how you focus on affordability. Now, when you say 2028, obviously, all of those people are not gonna be presidential contenders. Mamdani isn’t, for sure. But I do think they are starting to formulate a theory of the case. And then I think there’s people who are doing a really poor job. So [California Governor] Gavin Newsom, I think, is doing a pretty lousy job, because he’s doing sort of a performance art against Trump, sort of mocking Trump, which I think gets you some attaboys on social media, but I don’t think is showing the American public a real way forward. Rosin: Okay, wait. Slow this down a little bit. I’m trying to understand what it is exactly that you’re reading, like, what your scale is. Because in the first set of people who you said were really good, like, why shouldn’t I have a tiny heartbreak that what we’re saying is: The main qualification for a good leader these days is someone who’s good at social media? Longwell: Oh, well, again, that’s what I’m saying. I’m saying that they’re good at communicating. And I think that for Democrats, they’ve spent 10 years with Republicans just out-communicating them at every phase. At the presidential phase, Trump practices narrative dominance. Republicans feeling shut out from mainstream media; they built their own media ecosystem that’s now dominant. Democrats don’t have something that corresponds to that. And so I’m not saying that they just have to be good at social media. I’m saying that Democrats have to go through and survey why it is that they are not connecting with the American public, why they have lost the ability to be a multiracial, working-class coalition, and the Republican Party is kinda eating their lunch on that front. What I’m asking for is for Democrats to figure out how to be a team together, repair their brand by having a set of policy ideas that they’re circling around, and to refocus on people’s material well-being, ’cause this is what voters want in the focus groups, right? When I go through the policy, we can fight about all the little stuff, but the main thing voters are saying is, Stop focusing so much on these divisive social issues, and focus more on improving my material well-being. And that means education. That means health care. That means my job. And I think Democrats can’t just say, Affordability, affordability, affordability. They actually have to put a plan forward that has a theory of the case of what it means to be a Democrat going into the 2030s. Rosin: Right. Okay, that all sounds amazing. Here’s what’s tricky about it. So if narrative dominance is the goal, what we’ve seen as the right and MAGA has built an alternative universe of narrative dominance is that it’s involved two things: lies and cruelty. And so I’m wondering, how you can create an alternate world of narrative dominance which is effective—because people might say they don’t want you to focus on cultural issues, but that certainly helped the right build narrative dominance. The famous “Kamala is for they/them. We’re for you.” Focusing on cultural issues really gets attention. So how do you avoid some of the pitfalls of building this alternate universe, and yet do it effectively? Like, maybe be aggressive but not be cruel. How do you walk that line, and have you ever focus-grouped that or seen it done really well? Longwell: Sometimes, people think that all Trump voters are alike, that for all of them, the cruelty is the point. And for some of them, the cruelty is the point, but not for everybody. And you say, like, If Trump built it on lies and cruelty, what do you do differently? And I’m like, Well, you could build it on the truth and decency. And honestly, that’s a little bit of a novel concept right now for voters. I think they’re hungry for it. I think they’ve been starved for it. However, it does have to come with offense. They do want a fighter, man. They want somebody, and not just somebody who’s gonna fight Trump. This is a little bit of a mistake. Yes, they are tired of losing to these bad Republicans, but voters tell me all the time, We don’t want to bring a pillow to a gunfight anymore, but also, we don’t want to be like them. And one of the big disconnects for voters is: Voters keep saying, Democrats tell us that the Republicans are an existential threat to democracy, but they don’t act like it. They don’t behave like it. And so I think that if Pete Buttigieg, and Jon Ossoff, and Mamdani, and AOC, and a whole bunch of these other people started doing things together, started pitching themselves as a team, started showing up together—just think about it for a second, how different it would be if Democrats said, We’re gonna show up for each other. It’s happening a little bit in the states right now as people repair the damaged relationships from these tough primaries on the Democratic side. But it’s not happening everywhere. [Representative] Jasmine Crockett’s not doing anything for [the U.S. Senate candidate] James Talarico. And that, to me, is a real mistake, because I think Democrats need to start showing up together as a community that draws people to them, instead of this crazy faction that is always at each other’s throats. Rosin: Okay, so that’s the future for the Democrats that you wish for. And they’re all gonna try, and none of them are gonna be able to do what Donald Trump did, for the reasons that I stated earlier, which is that they do not have the same connection to voters that Donald Trump does. And so I think that Donald Trump brought so many new people into the system, and was so specific in the way he did it and the relationship that he had with voters, that the Republicans have no idea what they’re gonna do in a post-Trump world. And that’s why if Democrats can get there first with an affirmative vision, they have an opportunity to dominate in a way that puts MAGA in the ground for the foreseeable future. Rosin: Right. Okay. Can Trump Buy Back Americans’ Love at $5,000 a Piece? Guess We’ll Find Out.
2026-09-10

Can Trump Buy Back Americans’ Love at $5,000 a Piece? Guess We’ll Find Out.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The first Republican midterm convention had all the simulacra of a real political convention. There were seats assigned by state and indicated by tall signs on poles, there was a host of precisely timed and deeply forgettable speeches by elected officials, and, of course, there was a keynote speaker. But those were all window dressing to hide what it really was: just another Trump rally. There was news—or at least an eye-popping statement designed to make headlines: Donald Trump promised that, so long as Republicans retained control of both the House and Senate, every American would receive a $5,000 check from the U.S. government. It was a statement perfectly calibrated the next day in newspapers and local news broadcasts. That does not, however, mean it’s likely to happen. Republicans are a long shot to keep the House and far from a lock to hold on to the Senate. There are obvious policy issues with this, as it would cost over a trillion dollars. There are obvious political issues with it: Trump has had issues trying to rally fiscally conservative Republicans behind far less ambitious agenda items. It also was perfectly hedged as a proposal, conditioned only on the scenario that the GOP manages to defy political gravity in November. Still, it’s something new, at a time when Trump’s “Lake America”–style gimmicks have gone stale—a replacement to standard promises from Trump’s first term about building a wall on the U.S.–Mexico border, ending foreign wars, and having so much winning that “your head will spin.” For those more likely to hear about politics from eight-second TikTok clips than 800-word news articles, Trump made masturbation jokes. The speakers preceding him were disjointed and all making their own pitches for why Democrats were bad and Republicans were good. Some touted the merits of last year’s Republican spending bill, now on its third or fourth name ahead of the midterms. Others focused on the evils of Democrats with the remarks of far-left streamer Hasan Piker, who has become a surrogate for a number of Democratic candidates, receiving particular attention. Some were candidates themselves, delivering versions of their own stump speech, just like the array of speakers who proceed Trump at every rally as a way to tide over the restless crowd before the main event. (The most noteworthy speaker before Trump was a recorded video from John Fetterman, who introduced his Republican colleague Dave McCormick. The iconoclastic Pennsylvanian did not back Trump in remarks that seemed designed more to influence Sunday-show bookers than Keystone State swing voters.) And just as there are occasional celebrity cameos at a Trump rally, there was one at the midterm convention, where early-aughts comic Adam Carolla did a routine. When Trump finally took the stage, he spoke for nearly two hours, just like a rally. In the front of the crowd were many of the usual Trump superfans who flock to every rally in the same way that fans of the Grateful Dead used to follow the rock group across the country. The president even shouted them out as he spoke—“the Front-Row Joes” and, of course, “Brick Suit,” a MAGA devotee who wears a suit with a brick pattern on it to show his ardent support for building a border wall with Mexico. The speech itself often felt like a rally speech. It was filled with digressions (Trump took shots at targets ranging from a New York Times writer to Michael Dukakis), and often the president spent an extended stretch as a fainthearted surrogate, urging voters to support Texas Senate nominee Ken Paxton, despite the fact that Paxton dresses badly. But, of course, a mega Trump rally is what the GOP needs right now. Part of the modern role of political conventions is to fix the fractures that emerge within a political party during a presidential primary and signify that everyone is united in a common task. In Trump’s GOP, that’s not an issue. While a handful of moderate dissidents are tacitly tolerated as a matter of expedience, most other heretics have been driven from the tent. The party is united around Trump; the challenge is whether a significant portion of Republicans will vote for anyone besides Trump. The traditional utility of Trump rallies in competitive elections is to energize the MAGA base. At a time when Republican enthusiasm is lagging, that’s what this was about. As Trump told viewers that his wish was for voters to pretend that his name was on the ballot, this was about trying to energize his diehard loyalists—the people who were ready to storm the Capitol on Jan. 6 but didn’t bother to show up in the voting booth in 2022—to turn out in November. Trump rallies have long been effective at this in specific races—Trump’s recent appearance in Myrtle Beach before a primary election in South Carolina may have been enough to steer that race to his preferred candidate—but this was just one big nationalized Trump rally. It might help some, but for anyone hoping it’ll swing the midterms, it probably wouldn’t be prudent to bet an entire $5,000 check. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The IRS Gave ICE Some Information It Really Shouldn’t Have. There Will Be Consequences for All of Us.
2026-09-10

The IRS Gave ICE Some Information It Really Shouldn’t Have. There Will Be Consequences for All of Us.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. The IRS arguably holds Americans’ most sensitive information, more so than any other federal agency, and for the better part of 2025, it quietly handed over that information, to the tune of about 47,000 records, to the Department of Homeland Security. This was done to aid in President Donald Trump’s deportation agenda, but that doesn’t mean it was legal: An appeals court this week found that the IRS violated federal law when it turned over the addresses of folks it deemed to lack permanent legal status. Now the agency and the employees who participated in the illicit information sharing could face civil and criminal consequences, including lawsuits from people who were deported after the IRS delivered their information to DHS. The IRS is just one agency that got roped into cooperating with the Trump administration amid its immigration crackdown last year. Under normal circumstances, the IRS is forbidden from disclosing Americans’ tax information to anyone, including to other federal agencies and even within the executive branch, something Congress codified into federal law in the aftermath of Watergate. However, there are a few narrow exceptions, including active non-tax-related criminal investigations. This is where Trump’s DHS began scheming, crafting a memorandum of understanding that took advantage of a loophole in a federal statute. The memorandum stated that the IRS could provide undocumented taxpayers’ addresses because a person is technically subject to criminal investigation if they remain in the U.S. beyond 90 days after receiving a final order of removal. The MOU was so controversial it drove former Acting IRS Commissioner Melanie Krause, whom Trump appointed in early 2025, to resign in protest last April, alongside other top IRS leadership. Nevertheless, (now) former Immigration and Customs Enforcement Director Todd Lyons wasted no time after the IRS deal was struck, requesting the last known addresses of 1.28 million undocumented individuals late that June. By July 1, the IRS was giving its employees the green light to process Lyons’ requests. However, according to the U.S. Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, per federal law, that blanket request shouldn’t have been honored. Instead, ICE should have provided the IRS with specific information about its criminal investigations before the IRS shared any of its records, including the addresses DHS had on file for the specified individuals; however, that did not happen for more than 90 percent of DHS’s records requests. As the appeals court concluded, the IRS “never confirmed whether ICE request contained information plausibly reflecting ‘the name and address of the taxpayer.’ ” In fact, the judges suggested, ICE could have submitted requests with addresses like “ ‘Don’t Care 12345,’ or ‘00000,’ and still received a taxpayer’s address.” That’s not all. If it had followed the law, ICE would have identified a point of contact at the agency for each taxpayer’s criminal investigation. Instead, the court found, Lyons was listed for all 1.28 million records requests—which is “facially impossible,” the judges wrote. The agency also failed to provide a reason why a taxpayer’s address would be relevant to its investigation. The bottom line is that the IRS dramatically moved away from its long-standing policy of closely guarding taxpayers’ sensitive information and reviewing records requests on a case-by-case basis to “a mass, automated review of millions of records at the press of a button.” What happens now? For its actions, the government could “face steep civil and criminal consequences for willful disclosure of information,” in violation of federal tax law—but it’s largely up to Congress to decide if and how to exact those consequences. The House Ways and Means Committee or the Senate Finance Committee could hold oversight hearings to dig into what occurred here and chose to penalize the IRS by pulling its funding or imposing guardrails on its work. The appetite to do so likely depends largely on whether Democrats retake the House in the upcoming midterms. Even more significantly, everyone who was swept up in DHS’s probe of addresses and was deported or subjected to ICE because the IRS handed over their tax records now has standing to sue. It’s not clear how many individuals were deported directly from the two agencies’ MOU, but we do know that arrests and deportations across the country have skyrocketed. However, those who were directly affected and are no longer on U.S. soil likely face an uphill battle to pursue legal action against the Trump administration. There’s another way this could all come back to bite the IRS. The agency has for decades carefully built up trust within America’s immigrant population by pushing them to file their taxes, no matter their status, and promising that this will not trigger deportation. With a new tax season beginning on Jan. 1, “some people will be wary of filing their tax returns,” Martin Sheil, a former IRS investigator and supervisory special agent for the agency’s Criminal Investigation Division, told me. “From an IRS perspective, it is their mission to ensure and promote voluntary compliance with the Internal Revenue Code.” The whole situation underscores the IRS’s politically fraught mission, which for decades, Sheil said, heavily discouraged its employees from making any illegal disclosures—even inadvertent ones—because people need to feel confident that their tax records are being protected. “Then you get into a wrestling match between agencies and mission priorities,” Sheil said. “ICE feels like their No. 1 one priority, laid out by the president, is to scoop up illegal immigrants, and they want to use any means necessary to assist them in meeting their mission priority.” But all federal agencies are at risk if Americans suddenly stop complying with the IRS—a trend that should majorly worry Congress. Maintaining the confidence of taxpayers is integral to keeping the federal government’s lights on, particularly when you consider that undocumented workers pay an estimated $90 billion in federal, state, and local taxes, despite being ineligible for most social programs, including Social Security and Medicare. Even a 1-percentage-point loss among the overall U.S. population would slash federal revenue by $40 billion. Over the past year, everyone in the U.S., regardless of legal status, has personally witnessed or read about ICE’s heavy-handed tactics across the country and is now learning about the unlawful relationship the IRS struck up with DHS. That could cumulatively inhibit people from complying with tax law, and this ought to alarm Congress, the body that has not just the authority to step in and investigate how the IRS and DHS have been working together, but an obligation to do so. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction. If you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! The episode also delves into how the court’s conservative majority uses doctrinal head-fakes to ensure that the president always wins in court. In the Amicus bonus episode, Madiba K. Dennie, of Balls and Strikes, joins Dahlia to unpack the justices’ attempts to prevent their papers from going public and analyzes the broader message that sends about SCOTUS. House GOP Leader Mike Johnson failed to pass an amendment that would cap the number of Supreme Court justices at nine. Friend of Slate Alexis Romero, an economic and environmental justice attorney, argues that this cynical move demonstrates that “Republicans are so scared of the electorate voting for judicial reform” that they are willing to try to sign away their own authority on the matter. Trump’s executive order commanding the U.S. Postal Service to suppress mail voting is not actually the first time the federal government has attempted to control what gets mailed to Americans. Friend of Slate Steven Lubet, the Williams Memorial Professor Emeritus at Northwestern University Pritzker School of Law, explains how USPS was similarly weaponized after a postmaster refused to mail pamphlets highlighting the human-rights abuses perpetuated by slavery. The trial of Lindsay Clancy, the high-profile murder case that consumed the nation for weeks, ended in a mistrial last Friday. The jury was held up by a single juror, who refused to join his 11 fellow jurors in finding Clancy not guilty. I spoke with a trial attorney to understand how common a hung jury really is, why the judge in this case decided not to throw out the one unruly juror, and what the prosecution should do next. Trump has continuously violated the rights and interests of individual states for his personal and partisan political interests, an impeachable offense, argues friend of Slate Frank Bowman, a law professor and a former federal and state prosecutor. The president has specifically gone after blue states, following an unprecedented pattern that amounts to “an abuse of presidential power sufficiently egregious and disruptive of constitutional order to merit impeachment,” Bowman writes. The Surprising Moment MAGA’s Hold on America Broke
2026-09-10

The Surprising Moment MAGA’s Hold on America Broke

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Exactly one year ago, on a warm afternoon in north-central Utah, conservative activist Charlie Kirk was shot and killed, allegedly by a 22-year-old assassin who had set up a makeshift sniper’s nest on a nearby rooftop. Kirk was in the middle of one of the campus debate shows that had become his calling card during his decade-long career as a MAGA agitator, and as such, disturbing videos of the killing quickly spread across social media. If you’ve never seen the footage, I don’t recommend seeking it out. Kirk lolls his head back in a sickeningly unnatural motion; blood gushes from his neck; the crowd screams and scatters. It’s an astonishing act of violence, and Kirk was officially pronounced dead at the hospital two hours later. In the aftermath of the assassination, the MAGA commentariat instinctively sainted the newly martyred Kirk. There was good reason for this: Kirk was a victim of political violence, and victims of political violence are generally reified in the public record. At long last, Donald Trump had a bloody shirt to wave about. But when I survey the legacy of that fateful day in Utah from the vantage point of 2026, something else becomes clear. What conservatives thought they might harvest from the death of Kirk—a soft-power cultural mandate, broad bipartisan empathy, perhaps an assertion that it was liberals who were the real psychopaths in the room—never came to pass. Instead, ironically, I’ve concluded that Kirk’s killing represented the tipping point of Peak MAGAdom, a breaking of a certain fever. It had been less than a year since Trump had retaken the presidency—and unlike in 2017, in 2025 the masses were grappling with whether Trump’s movement was culturally ascendent. Kirk’s murder proved that it was not. How am I so sure about this? Well, for one thing, on the first anniversary of Charlie Kirk’s death, he has become, by far, the biggest meme on the internet. Where do we even start? How about the most offensive thing I’ve ever seen—we can work backward from there. Here is an A.I.-hewn video of Charlie Kirk at that pivotal Utah debate show. Portentous clouds gather overhead. The bullet is fired, and, miraculously, it is caught by someone inches away from its target. The camera pulls back to reveal who Kirk’s savior is. It’s Jeffrey Epstein. He and Kirk form a conga line in celebration. (This rendering was posted by X user @GlobalNewsMontr. As of this writing, it has been viewed more than 1.3 million times.) Zooming out further, here is a subreddit—called r/kirkified—that was created on Nov. 2, 2025, less than two months after the assassination. Inside, there are hundreds—thousands?—of images edited to paste Kirk’s distinctive gestalt into otherwise unrelated scenes. Here is Kirk as Napoleon Bonaparte. Here is Kirk as Gordon Freeman from the video game franchise Half-Life. Here is Kirk superimposed on the iconic McLovin ID from Superbad. “Kirkification”—and its syntactical variants—has gained real salience in the lexicon. You see it everywhere, with remarkable leaps in scale. For instance: Here is a Kirkified version of a scene from an Odyssey, where … well, where an unlucky soldier takes an arrow to the neck. (It’s sitting at 5.3 million views.) I could go on and on. Getting “Kirked” is now zoomer slang for getting killed. (It has also been deployed as a euphemism for oral sex. I don’t endorse any of this by the way—I am just reporting the facts.) Elsewhere, Kirk’s legacy has been desecrated to the point that it has shed even the thinnest connection to its original context. On a recent podcast clip, I heard the 22-year-old comedian and actress Ivy Wolk use the term “lowkirkuinely”—a portmanteau of “low-key” and “genuinely”—alloyed with the podcaster’s name. (“Just the name Charlie Kirk can send people of my generation into a fit of giggles,” said Wolk, explaining the humor.) You are free to find all of this to be pretty ghoulish. I can’t really blame you. Kirk left behind a widow and two young children. He was only 31 years old. The internet’s humor has a certain nihilism to it, and it’s fair for anyone to be turned off by that. (Remember, 9/11—which is also about to celebrate a big anniversary—is the subject of countless questionable memes as well.) But I do think something else is going on here, beyond typical online stuff. The derisiveness expressed toward Kirk—the permission so many people have given themselves to dance on his grave—feels, to me, in direct conversation with how Americans were instructed to process his untimely death, and that is one of the great unforced errors of the late and febrile Trump era. Seriously, remember what life felt like in the summer of 2025? It was the high-water mark of MAGAdom. Trump was back in office with his first and only popular vote victory under his belt. The tide of wokeness had been battled back into the abyss; companies and institutions cowered in front of this newly cemented moral majority. Liberals stared at themselves in the mirror, wondering how it all went so wrong, and some Democratic Party elites seemed eager to break right. (Here’s where I’ll remind you that shortly after the 2024 election, Gavin Newsom invited Kirk to his podcast studio.) So naturally, in the white heat of MAGA’s dominance, Kirk’s death resulted in him being feted like he was Nelson Mandela. Trump personally announced Kirk’s passing on his Twitter account and blessed him with the auspices of a state funeral. Republicans attempted to cobble together votes allowing the man to lie in state within the Capitol Rotunda; statues of Kirk were commissioned in MAGA strongholds; Vladimir Putin toasted his defense of “traditional values.” More importantly, the administration used Kirk to assert its political ambition, which we all thought was nearing its apogee. Stephen Miller announced a dubious crackdown on “far-left terror” networks, which felt like a naked extension of his paramilitary apparatus. And who could forget the whole Jimmy Kimmel debacle? The late-night host was yanked off TV for a spell, under direct pressure from the Federal Communications Commission—all because he had the temerity to make a mild joke at Kirk’s expense. It was those overreactions that, in retrospect, led to the latter-day blaspheming of Kirk—meted out in caustic memes, every day. Because no matter the horrific circumstances of his murder, Charlie Kirk is not Nelson Mandela. He went to his grave as the same man he always was: a bigoted podcaster who gleefully stoked hatred; someone who has antagonized innumerable minority groups, who helped bus rioters to the “Stop the Steal” rally, who, in fact, spent his last moments on Earth making a flagrantly racist comment about gang violence. None of that justified his fate—not even close—but it did make Kirk an easily recognizable archetype in the MAGA strata: an individual well-versed in the shabby right-wing grift, a gigantic microphone in front of his smirking face, permeating the airwaves with his poisonous worldview. The sacredness prescribed by the Trump administration—asking us to reserve the same solemnity for Kirk as we did for popes, firefighters, and veterans, which the president so rarely offers his own enemies—was a bridge too far. It turns out that millions of Americans refused to lie to themselves about who Kirk was. And as the MAGA machine attempted to inculcate respect for the departed podcaster through raw vehicles of coercion, the spirit of rebellion grew more potent. Ultimately, I think that is what a Kirkified meme attempts to express: the right to memorialize the man in any way we see fit. Two months after Kirk’s passing, a song titled “We Are Charlie Kirk” was uploaded by the YouTube channel “Authentic Music.” It was presented as a tribute to the late podcaster; the chorus speaks of “carrying the flame,” and “honoring his name.” It was also, clearly, the product of A.I., and like all A.I. music, the results were turgid, saccharine, and spiritually vacant—reminiscent of an Imagine Dragons B-side lost to time. Unsurprisingly, the song was quickly repurposed as a Kirk meme, and in that context, its lyrics have taken on a completely different meaning. The memers will ensure that the world never forgets who Charlie Kirk really was. They will shatter the MAGA propaganda machine. They will carry the flame.
